Statement setting out material facts pursuant to Section 102 of the Companies Act, 2013
Item No. 4:
The Board of Directors of the Company, based on the 
recommendation of the Audit Committee, has approved the appointment of M/s. Ashwin Solanki & Associates, Cost Accountant (Registration No.: 100392) as Cost Auditor at their Meeting held on May 25, 2026, to conduct the audit of the cost records of the Company for the financial year ending March 31, 2027. In accordance with the provisions of Section 148 of the Companies Act, 2013 (“the Act”) read with the Companies (Audit and Auditors) Rules, 2014, the remuneration payable to the Cost Auditors requires to be approved by the Members of the Company.
Accordingly, approval of the Members is sought as 
referred to in the Resolution No. 4 of the Notice for the payment of the remuneration amounting to `  24,00,000/- (Rupees Twenty Four Lakhs only), for Cost Audit plus applicable taxes and out of pocket expenses incurred during the course of audit for the financial year ending March 31, 2027.
The Board of Directors recommends the Resolution for 
approval of Members as an Ordinary Resolution as set out in Resolution No. 4 of the accompanying Notice, considering the continued association of M/s. Ashwin Solanki & Associates, Cost Accountant with the Company.
None of the Directors and/or Key Managerial Personnel 
of the Company and / or their respective relatives is concerned or interested, financially or otherwise, in the Ordinary Resolution as set out in Resolution No. 4 of the Notice.
Item No. 5:
Hitachi Japan, the ultimate holding company of Hitachi Energy India Limited (the “Company”) proposes to launch 
the Hitachi Group Employee Stock Purchase Plan (“ESPP”) and Restricted Stock Units Plan (“RSU”) for eligible employees of the companies across various jurisdictions.
ESPP purpose: RSU purpose:
 • Encourage long-term employee share ownership and engagement;
 • Align employee interests with the long-term value creation of the Hitachi Group (i.e., Hitachi Ltd, Japan and its subsidiaries); and
 • Provide employees with an opportunity to participate in the equity of the ultimate holding company. • The RSU is granted to executives/ leaders of several countries across subsidiaries of the Hitachi Japan.
 • The RSU strengthens the connection between leadership decisions and Hitachi’s long-term corporate value for its customers, shareholders, employees  and society.
 • The RSU also aligns leadership decisions closely with the Company’s long-term success.
 • RSU recipients are expected to act with a shareholder mindset, considering how today’s actions contribute to sustainable growth and value creation.
RSU’s will replace the existing cash-based Long-Term 
Incentive Plan (LTIP) for eligible employees. The RSU has a 3-year vesting period, with grants vesting in three annual tranches.
Unless defined herein, capitalized terms shall have the same meaning as defined in the ESPP and RSU rules.
Within the J.P. Morgan Workplace Solutions structure, 
Global Shares Execution Services Limited (“GSESL”) - a Markets in Financial Instruments Directive (MiFID)-compliant, regulated entity within the JPMorgan Chase group - opens an online account for employees to enroll in the ESPP in accordance with the ESPP and to grant the RSU to eligible employees who accept GSESL’s Terms & Conditions. 
142
Integrated Annual Report 2025-26w 
=== page 146 ===
ESPP management and administration: RSU management and administration:
For the ESPP, GSESL submits purchase and any sell orders to its 
execution broker partner, Instinet (a Nomura group company) 
and holds shares on behalf of the underlying beneficiaries 
(participants residing outside Japan) in a nominee account 
with its global custodian, CACEIS Bank (as the registered legal 
shareholder), through its local custodian, Citibank Tokyo, 
which has access to Japan Securities Depository Center, Inc. 
(JASDEC), the Japanese central securities depository.For the RSU, the trustee appointed by Hitachi Japan transfers 
the shares to J.P. Morgan at vesting for all participants residing 
outside Japan. GSESL submits any sell orders to its execution 
broker partner, Instinet (a Nomura group company) and holds 
shares on behalf of the underlying beneficiaries (participants 
residing outside Japan) in a nominee account with its global 
custodian, CACEIS Bank (as the registered legal shareholder), 
through its local custodian, Citibank Tokyo, which has access to 
Japan Securities Depository Center, Inc. (JASDEC), the Japanese 
central securities depository.
ESPP structure:
If an Eligible Employee enrolls in the ESPP, the Company 
provides a corresponding cash matching payment 
("Employer Contribution") equal to 15% of the Participant 
Contribution ("Employer Contribution Percentage"). The 
Company deducts the Participant Contribution from the 
net salary and pays the Employer Contribution in the 
local payroll, subject to income tax deductions as may 
be applicable. Thereafter, the Participant Contribution 
and the Employer Contribution are transferred to the 
plan administrator to buy the shares in the name of each 
Participant at the market price.
Under the ESPP, along with other Indian participating 
Hitachi Group companies, the Employer Contribution 
of 15% represents the financial assistance provided 
by the Company to Eligible Employees who voluntarily 
decide to enroll in the ESPP. This contribution enables 
participating employees to acquire a greater number 
of shares during each ESPP cycle, which generally 
comprises the accumulation of Participant Contributions 
and Employer Contributions. The Participant Contribution 
and Employer Contribution will be fixed in INR during the 
enrollment process and remain unchanged during the 
ESPP cycle. For the avoidance of doubt, the Participant 
Contribution is the employee’s own salary deduction and 
does not constitute financial assistance under Section 67 
of the Act and only the Employer Contribution constitutes 
financial assistance for the purposes of Section 67 of 
the Act.
Hitachi Japan or applicable Authorized Body thereof can 
at its discretion change the Participant Contribution limits 
and Employer Contribution Percentage or terminate and 
amend the rules of the ESPP from time to time.
RSU structure:
The Company provides the list of eligible employees, 
with due approval / recommendation of Nomination 
and Remuneration Committee of Directors and Board of 
Directors in the event of Senior Management or KMP’s, as may be applicable and the RSU target amounts per 
individual for the global equity compensation team to 
calculate the units to grant. Participants receive a Grant 
Statement confirming the grant date, currency exchange 
rate and share price applied at grant, total units granted 
and units vesting under each tranche and the vesting 
timing of each tranche. Participants will also receive the 
vesting statements.
Please note that all grants, vesting and share releases 
of the RSU awards are subject to the approval of the 
relevant Authorized Body and Hitachi Japan may amend 
the rules of the RSU from time to time, subject to the 
approval of the relevant Authorized Body.
According to Section 67 of the Companies Act, 2013 
(“Act”), no public company is permitted to give, 
whether directly or indirectly and whether through a 
loan, guarantee, the provision of security or otherwise, 
any financial assistance for, or in connection with, the 
purchase or subscription made or to be made, by any 
person of or for any shares in the company or its holding 
company unless inter-alia  the same is approved by the 
Members by way of a Special Resolution. As prescribed 
under Section 67(3) of the Act, read with Rule 16 of 
the Companies (Share Capital and Debentures) Rules, 
2014 (“Rules”) made thereunder, as amended from time 
to time, the aggregate value of the financial assistance 
provided by the Company (being the Employer 
Contribution) together with the value of shares to be 
purchased with the Participant Contribution under the 
ESPP and RSU shall not exceed five per cent (5%) of the 
Company’s aggregate paid-up capital and free reserves. 
The Company shall monitor and ensure ongoing 
compliance with this statutory limit.
Further, the information set out below regarding the 
ESPP and RSU is provided for the reference of Members:

a)  C lass of Employees for whose benefit Hitachi Group ESPP and RSU is being implemented; Loan/finance

Under the ESPP, the Company will provide financial 
assistance in the form of Employer Contribution only to Participants.
For example, if the Participant Contribution is ` 47,000 per 
month, the Employer Contribution will be ` 7,050 per month 
subject to tax deductions as applicable. The Company will transfer ` 47,000 plus ` 7,050 to the plan administrator to 
buy shares of Hitachi Japan at the market price. Participants can withdraw from ESPP at any time.Under the RSU, the grant occurs annually according to the Company’s eligibility criteria.

term contract employed by the Company for the RSU grant, according to the applicable eligibility criteria, as amended from time to time.

senior management who are on a permanent or fixed term contract for the RSU grant from time to time.

egular employees such as Trainees, casual workers,

b) R eservation periods:
ESPP reservation period RSU reservation period
All Eligible Employees who wish to participate must submit their enrollment within the timeline specified as part of the ESPP communication campaign.The RSU grant occurs annually according to the Company’s eligibility criteria and subject to the approval of the relevant Authorized Body.
c) Subscrip tion Price or Pricing Formula:
ESPP subscription RSU subscription
Participants can elect how much from their net salary they wish to contribute each month to buy shares of Hitachi Japan within the applicable minimum and maximum limits (“Participant Contribution”), with the Employer Contribution capped at 15% thereof. GSESL will buy the shares at the market price on behalf of the Participants each month.The RSU grant depends on the percentage of the annual base salary the Company defines based on market benchmarks. The resulting amount will be converted to JPY and used to calculate the RSU units to grant. The units granted will vest according to the RSU plan rules, subject to continuous employment conditions.

ESPP payment method RSU payment method

o)  Details about who would exercise and how 
the voting rights in respect of the shares to be 
purchased or subscribed to under the scheme 
would be exercised:
  Under Japanese law, to vote, ESPP and RSU 
Participants must hold one hundred (100) common 
shares or more on the AGM record date. 100 
shares constitute one trading unit (also known as 
a 'round lot'). Shares held in quantities of less than 
one trading unit do not confer voting rights at the 
AGM (also known as an "odd lot" of 1-99 share 
units); and to vote, shareholders must be listed in 
the shareholder register on the AGM record date.
  Shareholders not listed in the shareholder register 
on the AGM record date may not vote at the AGM. 
ESPP and RSU Participants who live outside Japan 
(non-Japanese resident) and meet the conditions 
to vote, the nominee will exercise voting rights on 
their behalf at the AGM. ESPP and RSU Participants 
can express their intentions for each proxy item 
by selecting For, Against, or Abstain on the AGM 
voting module available from the account they have with the plan administrator. The plan submits the 
voting results to their custodian partner. Any shares 
not used to vote are treated as unexercised in the 
final submission.
  Further, in accordance with Rule 16(4) of the 
Companies (Share Capital and Debentures) 
Rules, 2014, in the event that voting rights in 
respect of shares held under the ESPP or RSU 
are not exercised directly by the ESPP and RSU 
Participants (as the nominee exercises voting rights 
on behalf of non-Japanese resident ESPP and RSU 
Participants), the Board of Directors shall make 
the requisite disclosures in the Board’s Report for 
the relevant financial year, including the names of 
such Participants, reasons for not voting directly, 
particulars of the person exercising such voting 
rights, the number and percentage of shares held, 
details of general meetings where voting power was 
exercised, the resolutions on which votes were cast, 
the percentage of such voting power to the total 
voting power on each resolution and whether the 
votes were cast in favor of or against the resolution.
p) Detailed disclosures with respect to the employee wise grant of stock options:
ESPP participation RSU participation
Under the ESPP, Participants can choose how much 
they want to contribute to buy shares monthly. Currently, 
the minimum monthly contribution is JPY 1’000 and 
the maximum monthly contribution is JPY 100'000. 
The Employer Contribution is capped at 15% of the 
Participant Contribution.The RSU grant occurs annually according to the 
Company’s eligibility criteria and subject to the 
approval of the relevant Authorized Body of Hitachi 
Japan and/ or the Board of Directors of the Company, 
the Nomination and Remuneration Committee or such 
other committee(s) as the Board of the Company may 
authorize and the shareholders of the Company, as 
applicable from time to time.
RSU participants receive shares in 3 tranches over 
the vesting period of 3 years, subject to continuous 
employment conditions.
  The aforesaid Statement covers relevant disclosures 
as required under Section 67(3) of the Act read with 
Rule 16 of the prescribed Rules.
  The Company confirms that as on March 31, 2026, 
the aggregate paid-up share capital of the Company 
is ` 8.91 Crores and the free reserves of the Company 
are ` 5,168.42 Crores. Accordingly, the maximum 
amount of financial assistance under the ESPP/ RSU 
(including the value of shares to be purchased) that 
may be provided under Section 67(3) of the Act 
read with Rule 16 of the Rules is ` 258.87 Crores 
(being 5% of the aggregate of paid-up share capital 
and free reserves).
  The Members may note that in terms of Section 67(2) 
of the Act and the rules made thereunder, a company 
must obtain prior approval from the shareholders 
before providing financial assistance to employees 
for subscribing to any shares of the company or 
its holding company, as proposed in Item No. 5 of 
this Notice.  In terms of Section 67 of the Companies Act, 
2013 read with Rule 16 of the Companies (Share 
Capital and Debentures) Rules, 2014 and other 
applicable rules made thereunder, if any (including 
any amendments or modifications thereto or re-
enactment thereof for the time being in force),the 
Company cannot provide finance facility/assistance 
or Employer Contribution to Participants to buy 
shares in Hitachi Japan under ESPP/ RSU unless 
the shareholders’ approval has been obtained. 
Accordingly, the Company has proposed to seek 
shareholders’ approval to avail the facility of the 
finance facility/assistance to ESPP Participants in the 
form of the Employer Contribution for purchasing the 
shares of the Ultimate Holding Company (directly/
indirectly).
  There will be no financial assistance provided by the 
Company in connection with the RSU scheme, other 
than determining employee eligibility based on the 
criteria set out above.

2024-25. Consequent to the aforesaid allotment,

71.31% thereby increasing the public shareholding 
from 25% to 28.69%.

10. CREDIT RATING:
 The Company had no outstanding borrowings as 
on March 31, 2026. Accordingly, no fund-based 
limits were utilized from the established credit lines 
with banks.
 CRISIL Ratings Limited has reaffirmed the long-term 
and short-term credit ratings for ` 6,000 Crores 
bank loan facilities of the Company.
 CRISIL has assigned ‘CRISIL A1+’ as Short-Term 
Rating and assigned ‘CRISIL AAA/Stable’ ratings 
as a Long-Term Rating effective from October 17, 
2024. CRISIL reaffirmed Long Term ratings as ‘CRISIL 
AAA/Stable’ as on March 24, 2025, an event driven 
review followed by QIP of equity shares issuance.
 The Company’s financial discipline and prudence 
are reflected in the strong credit ratings ascribed 
by rating agencies. The details of credit ratings 
are also disclosed in the Management Discussion 
and Analysis section, which forms part of the

  Selection and Procedure for Nomination and 
Appointment of Directors and Nomination and 
Remuneration Policy of the Company:
 The Nomination and Remuneration Committee 
(‘NRC’) of the Company is entrusted to determine 
the criteria for the requirements of the Board. NRC 
while recommending candidatures to the Board, 
takes into consideration the qualification, attributes, 
experience and independence of the candidate.
 Pursuant to Section 178(3) of the Act, the NRC of 
the Board has formulated, amongst others, a Policy 
on Nomination and Remuneration which provides 
the framework for remunerating the members of 
the Board, KMP, Senior Management and other 
employees of the Company. This Policy is guided 
by the principles and objectives enumerated in  
Section 178(4) of the Act.
 The details of the Nomination and Remuneration 
Policy are mentioned in the report on Corporate 
Governance and the same is also placed on the 
Company’s website at https://www.hitachienergy.
com/in/en/investor-relations/board-of-directors.
 Disclosures pertaining to Remuneration and other 
details as required under Section 197(12) of the Act 
read with Rule 5(1) of the Companies (Appointment 
and Remuneration of Managerial Personnel) Rules, 
2014 are provided in the prescribed format and 
annexed as Annexure-B to this Report.  Annual Performance Evaluation of the Board, its 
Committees and individual Directors:
 The Board, along with the NRC, approved a criteria 
framework in the form of a questionnaire for annual 
evaluation of the Board, Board Committees and 
Individual Directors pursuant to the provisions of 
the Act and the Corporate Governance requirements 
under Regulation 25(4) of the SEBI Listing 
Regulations read with SEBI’s Guidance Note on 
Board Evaluation.
 During the year under review, the Board of Directors 
has carried out an annual evaluation of its own 
performance, Board Committees and Individual 
Directors. The aforementioned annual performance 
evaluation was carried out through a digitized 
questionnaire-based survey administered via a 
secure electronic portal. The evaluation framework 
covered qualitative parameters and sought feedback 
through ratings.
 The evaluation questionnaire/ survey was 
circulated electronically to all the Board members 
of the Company in a transparent and confidential 
manner. The key parameters considered for Board 
evaluation were Board Membership, Board’s 
Culture and Relationships with Key Constituencies, 
Board Responsibilities, Decision Making and Board 
Committees. During the evaluation process, the 
Directors have given ratings of either ‘Strongly 
Agree’ / ‘Agree’ on various assessment questions, 
through the portal.
 Further, the performance evaluation of the 
Independent Directors was carried out by the entire 
Board. The performance evaluation of the Chairman, 
the Board as a whole and the Non-Independent 
Directors was carried out by the Independent 
Directors at their separate Meeting held during 
the year.
 A consolidated report was shared with the Chairman 
of the Board for his review. Based on the outcomes 
of the evaluation, appropriate feedback was 
provided to the Directors.

30. WHISTLEBLOWER POLICY/ VIGIL MECHANISM: 
 Pursuant to Section 177(9) of the Act and 
Regulation 22 of the SEBI Listing Regulations, the 
Company has adopted a Whistleblower Policy/ 
Vigil Mechanism for Directors, Employees and third 
parties to report their concerns about unethical or 
inappropriate behavior, actual or suspected fraud or 
violation of the Company’s Code of Conduct, leak of 
unpublished price sensitive information and related 
matters.  
 This mechanism also provides adequate safeguards 
against the victimization of whistleblowers who 
avail of the whistleblower / vigil mechanism. 
Whistleblowers also have access to senior 
management and/or the Audit Committee. The 
Whistleblower Policy is available on the Company’s 
website at https://www.hitachienergy.com/
in/en/about-us/integrity/reporting-channels/
whistleblower-protection-policy .  
 During the year under review, the Company received 
a total of 18 cases, of which 14 have been closed 
and 4 are currently under investigation as on date 
of this report.
31. RISK MANAGEMENT POLICY: 
 The Company has in place the Risk Management 
Policy and constituted the Risk Management 
Committee as required under the Act and Regulation 
21 of the SEBI Listing Regulations. The Committee is 
chaired by an Independent Director, which assists the 
Board in monitoring and overseeing implementation 
of the Risk Management Policy, including evaluating 
the adequacy of risk management systems and such 
other functions as mandated under the SEBI Listing 
Regulations and as the Board may deem fit from 
time to time.
 The Committee oversees the Risk Management 
process including risk identification, impact 
assessment, effective implementation of the 
mitigation plans and risk reporting. The purpose of 
the Committee is to assist the Board of Directors in 
fulfilling its oversight responsibilities with regard to 
enterprise risk management.

Annexure-A to Board’s Report
Management Discussion & Analysis
Economic overview: India’s energy-led 
growth story
India economy
This year marked a perceptible inflection point in the 
Indian economy – a dynamic geopolitical environment, 
exponential growth in the energy requirements of the 
country and a supportive regulatory and policy ecosystem 
put energy security at the heart of economic growth.
Rising manufacturing capabilities, digitalization, 
transportation electrification, infrastructure expansion 
and urbanization continue to shape investments across 
the power value chain. At the same time, efforts to 
diversify energy sources and strengthen domestic 
potential are supporting the country’s broader objective 
of enhancing energy security and reducing exposure to 
external disruptions.
The geopolitical dimension of energy is now intersecting 
with another defining force of this time - technology. 
India’s digital economy is scaling rapidly. Artificial 
intelligence, cloud computing and data centers are 
fueling electricity demand. Unlike traditional industries, 
these sectors require uninterrupted quality power. In this 
context, energy is becoming a competitive differentiator. 
India is clearly on route to gradually emerging as an 
‘electrostate’.
The Government’s continued focus on strengthening 
the power sector through policy support, infrastructure 
development initiatives and measures aimed at 
accelerating the energy transition, has created a 
conducive environment for long-term sectoral growth. 
Trade and economic cooperation agreements with 
global partners are also supporting technology access, 
investment flows and the development of a more 
integrated energy ecosystem.
India’s energy transition agenda remains centered on 
expanding the share of cleaner energy sources while 
ensuring a reliable and affordable power supply. As the 
energy mix evolves, greater emphasis is being placed on 
transmission infrastructure, grid modernization, energy 
storage and digital technologies to support system 
flexibility and maintain grid stability.
With a balanced focus on economic growth, energy 
security and decarbonization, India continues to 
strengthen the foundations of a more sustainable and 
resilient energy ecosystem. These developments are 
expected to support long-term investments across the power sector and create opportunities for technologies 
and innovation that enable a secure, efficient and low-
carbon energy future. This is a powerful contributor to 
the country’s economic growth amongst other factors.
The outlook for the Indian economy remains positive 
and stable, with real GDP growth. GDP grew by 7.6% in 
FY 2025-26 vis-à-vis 7.1% in FY 2024-25, supported 
by strong domestic demand, income tax exemptions, GST 
rationalization and an accommodative monetary policy. 
In a global outlook increasingly shadowed by the conflict 
in West Asia, the International Monetary Fund (IMF) 
positions India as one of the few resilient economies.

The country’s growth trajectory continues to be 
supported by rapid urbanization, expanding infrastructure

expand and economic activity broadens across sectors, 
the need for reliable, efficient and sustainable power 
infrastructure has become increasingly important to 
support long-term economic development.
Sustained government infrastructure expenditure, 
alongside steadily rising private sector investments, will 
anchor this expansion, complemented by a strengthening 
manufacturing foundation. The services sector anticipates 
continued consistent performance, reinforcing structural 
economic resilience.
India’s energy sector sits at the heart of its economic 
transformation, shaped by a powerful convergence of 
sustained GDP growth, structural demand expansion, 
progressive policy support and an accelerating 
sustainability agenda.FRE = First Revised Estimate; SAE = Second Revised Estimate;

Indian power sector development 1,2,3
India’s power sector is undergoing a once-in-a-
generation transformation, driven by structural demand growth, policy acceleration and a decisive shift toward electrification and decarbonization. The sector today represents a large and expanding total addressable market (TAM), spanning generation, transmission and distribution networks, emerging technologies and end-use industries.
FY 2025–26 marked a defining year for the sector, 
reflecting both resilience and rapid structural progress. India successfully met a peak power demand of 242.49 GW, while energy shortages declined sharply to 0.03%, indicating strong supply reliability. Power availability has improved significantly across the country – rural supply increased from 12.5 hours in 2014 to 22.6 hours in 2025, while urban areas now enjoy over 23 hours of daily supply, highlighting near-universal access and enhanced service quality.
Two major milestones underscore the scale of 
this transformation:
 •Total installed power capacity surpassed 520 GW as of March 2026 – more than doubling 
from 249 GW in 2014.
 •Renewable energy crossed 51% of electricity demand at peak levels, marking a significant step toward a cleaner and more resilient energy system.These achievements reflect sustained policy support, large-scale investments and coordinated execution across the power ecosystem.
India is firmly on the path to becoming a global energy 
leader with its groundbreaking initiatives such as universal electrification, enhanced rural power availability and the adoption of cutting-edge technologies.
Generation, including renewable energy
6
The generation segment continues to be the cornerstone 
of India’s power sector, underpinning the country’s economic growth and rising energy demand. Over the past decade, the sector has witnessed sustained capacity addition alongside a structural shift in its energy mix. While thermal power remains essential for providing baseload stability and ensuring grid reliability, the growth trajectory of the sector is increasingly being shaped by renewable energy, particularly solar and wind, driven by strong policy support, competitive auction mechanisms and improving cost economics.
India’s electricity generation has expanded significantly 
from 1,168 billion units (BU) in FY 2015–16 to 1,845.9 BU in FY 2025–26, reflecting robust demand growth across industrial, commercial and residential segments. In parallel, the country’s installed power capacity increased from 305 GW to 520 GW over the same period, highlighting the scale of infrastructure development and investment across the generation value chain. This expansion has been broad-based, with a clear increase in the contribution of non-fossil fuel sources, indicating a gradual but decisive transition toward a more diversified and sustainable energy mix.

Hydro, nuclear and other sources together provide additional diversification. This changing composition underscores the dual approach being followed by the sector – maintaining conventional capacity for stability while rapidly scaling up clean energy to meet long-term sustainability objectives.

This transition is even more evident in the generation mix. Total power generation in FY 2025–26 stood at 1,845.9 BU, with fossil fuels contributing 1,306.9 BU, or 70.8% of the total and non-fossil sources accounting for 538.9 BU, or 29.2%. Notably, while fossil fuel-based generation declined by 4.1% year-on-year, non-fossil generation grew by 15.7%, driven by strong growth across renewable segments. Solar and wind generation increased by over 20% and 27% respectively, while hydro and bioenergy also recorded steady growth. This divergence reflects a clear structural shift, with renewable energy increasingly contributing to incremental generation.The Rising Share of Renewable Energy: India at the Forefront of the Global Transition
A new energy paradigm is unfolding globally, with 
renewable energy rapidly increasing its share in the power mix, driven by decarbonization imperatives, energy security concerns and technological advancements. Within this global transition, India has emerged as one of the most dynamic and influential markets, combining scale, speed and policy ambition to redefine its energy landscape.
India’s renewable energy journey is gaining unprecedented 
momentum, supported by strong government push, rising electricity demand and a strategic focus on sustainability and energy independence. The country recorded a landmark addition of 55.29 GW of renewable capacity in FY 2025–26, underscoring the accelerating shift toward clean energy and positioning renewables at the center of its power sector transformation.
This rapid expansion has firmly established India as a 
global clean energy leader, ranking 3
rd in solar power 
capacity, 4th in wind power and 4th in overall renewable 
energy capacity globally. As of March 31, 2026, India’s total installed power capacity stood at 520 GW, of which 283.46 GW was contributed by non-fossil fuel sources, including approximately 274 GW from renewable energy. The renewable portfolio is led by 150.26 GW of solar capacity, followed by 56.09 GW of wind power and over 51 GW of hydro power, along with contributions from bioenergy and small hydro.
This structural shift reflects the increasing dominance of 
clean energy within India’s power system. Renewables are expected to overtake coal-fired generation as the leading source of electricity as early as CY 2025 or CY 2026, marking a historic inflection point in the country’s energy mix. During FY 2025–26, India also achieved its highest-ever renewable share in electricity generation, with renewables meeting over 51% of total power demand at peak levels, highlighting both capacity scale and system integration capability.
At a global level, the transition is equally pronounced. 
Wind and solar generation surpassed 5,000 TWh in CY 2025 and is expected to exceed 6,000 TWh in CY 2026, with solar PV accounting for nearly 80% of incremental additions. The combined share of solar and wind in global electricity generation is projected to rise from around 15% in CY 2024 to 20% by CY 2026, reflecting the fastest growth in any energy segment over the past decade.
India’s progress in renewable energy is not only about 
capacity expansion but also about achieving broader

strategic objectives. The increasing share of non-fossil 
fuel sources signals a decisive move toward reducing 
fossil fuel dependency, strengthening energy security 
and building a more resilient energy framework. 
Importantly, this transition has been achieved alongside 
rising electricity demand, demonstrating the robustness 
and scalability of India’s energy system.
The country’s achievements are also reflected in its 
climate commitments. India has already met one of its 
key COP26 (Glasgow, 2021) “Panchamrit” targets – to 
achieve 50% of installed power capacity from non-fossil 
fuel sources by 2030 – five years ahead of schedule. 
This early milestone underscores the effectiveness of 
sustained policy support, strong institutional coordination 
and large-scale investment across the energy value chain.
In parallel, India continues to strengthen its energy 
resilience. As the third-largest producer and consumer of 
electricity globally, the country plays a central role in the 
global energy transition, with its share in global primary 
energy demand expected to rise significantly over the next 
decade. Improvements in power availability, reduction in 
shortages – from 4.2% in FY 2013–14 to near-negligible 
levels in recent years—and near-universal electrification 
have reinforced the reliability and inclusiveness of the 
power system.
Overall, the increasing share of renewable energy in India’s 
power mix reflects a deeper structural transformation. It 
highlights the country’s ability to align economic growth 
with sustainability imperatives, scale clean energy at an 
unprecedented pace and build a resilient, future-ready 
energy ecosystem.
India is not only participating in the global energy 
transition – it is actively shaping it.
Transmission: Enabling the energy transition 5,6,7
The transmission sector is emerging as a critical enabler 
of India’s energy transition, poised for significant scale-
up. This growth is underpinned by rising peak power 
demand, rapid renewable energy additions and the 
targets outlined in the National Electricity Plan (NEP) 
2022–32. As renewable capacity expands – often in 
resource-rich yet remote locations – it is driving the 
need for substantial investments in high-capacity, long-
distance transmission corridors.
Structural enablers such as Green Energy Corridors, 
stronger grid interconnections and enhanced cross-
regional transfer capabilities are expanding the 
addressable opportunity. At the same time, the sector is 
seeing increasing emphasis on grid reliability, efficiency 
and digitalization. The Tariff-Based Competitive Bidding 
(TBCB) framework has further transformed the sector, driving competitive intensity, improving project quality 
and accelerating execution timelines.
The sector recorded robust growth in FY 2025–26, 
led by large-scale renewable energy evacuation 
projects and continued strengthening of both the Inter-
State Transmission System (ISTS) and state networks. 
Significant commissioning of 765 kV and 400 kV 
corridors connected key renewable hubs such as Khavda 
(Gujarat) and Bhadla (Rajasthan) to major demand 
centers. Notably, over 2,300 km of 765 kV transmission 
lines were commissioned during the year, reinforcing the 
backbone of India’s high-capacity national grid.
While government initiatives such as the Green Energy 
Corridor (GEC) and the General Network Access (GNA) 
framework have accelerated transmission buildout, 
there remains scope to further fast-track greenfield 
development through structured Public-Private 
Partnership (PPP) models. Such approaches can enable 
more effective risk-sharing and faster execution.
These efforts are aligned with the government’s 
“One Grid, One Nation” vision, aimed at building a 
unified, resilient and seamlessly integrated national 
transmission network.
Distribution: Reforms and efficiency imperative
The distribution segment remains central to the sector’s 
value chain, with ongoing reforms aimed at improving 
operational efficiency and financial sustainability. Smart 
metering, loss reduction initiatives and regulatory 
interventions are transforming the segment. As 
consumption scales and end-user expectations evolve, 
distribution utilities are emerging as key drivers of 
demand visibility and service innovation.
Energy storage: A new growth frontier
Energy storage is rapidly emerging as one of the 
most compelling growth segments within the power 
ecosystem, playing a critical role in addressing renewable 
energy intermittency and enhancing grid reliability. 
Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) are witnessing 
accelerated deployment driven by falling costs, policy 
support and the need for flexible, fast-response capacity.Structural enablers such as Green 
Energy Corridors, stronger grid 
interconnections and enhanced 
cross-regional transfer capabilities are 
expanding the addressable opportunity.

At the same time, Pumped Storage Projects (PSPs) 
are gaining renewed momentum as viable large-scale, long-duration storage solutions, particularly suited for balancing peak demand and supporting high renewable penetration. In parallel, green hydrogen is evolving as a strategic pillar in the energy transition, offering significant potential for industrial decarbonization and positioning India as a future export hub.
Mobility: Rail, Metro and Electric vehicles
The electrification of mobility is emerging as a powerful 
structural driver of electricity demand in India. Large-scale initiatives such as Indian Railways’ ongoing electrification and the rapid expansion of metro rail networks across urban centers are significantly increasing the reliance on electric power for transportation.
In parallel, the accelerating adoption of electric vehicles 
(EVs) is creating a new and fast-growing demand segment. The build-out of EV charging infrastructure represents a scalable and distributed load category, unlocking opportunities across the entire power value chain—from generation and transmission to distribution and smart grid management.
As mobility transitions toward cleaner energy sources, it 
is not only driving incremental demand but also reshaping load patterns, necessitating investments in grid flexibility, storage integration and digital energy management solutions. This evolving landscape positions the power sector as a central enabler of sustainable mobility and long-term decarbonization.
Data centres: The digital demand multiplier 
8,9,10
The rapid growth of data centers is introducing a 
structurally new and high-intensity demand segment within the power sector. Driven by accelerating digitization, cloud adoption and the rise of AI-led workloads, data centers are evolving into one of the fastest-growing electricity consumers globally and in India.
India’s data center market is estimated at USD 11.76 
billion, growing from USD 10.11 billion in CY2025 and is projected to expand at a CAGR of 16.3% to reach USD 25.07 billion by 2031. In parallel, IT load capacity is expected to increase sharply from ~4.5 GW in 2025 to ~12.5 GW by 2030, reflecting a robust ~22.7% CAGR over the period.
This growth is translating into substantial power demand 
expansion. Installed data center power capacity is expected to double from ~1 GW currently to ~2 GW by 2026 and further scale up to over 8 GW by 2030, implying a near five-fold increase. As a result, data centers are projected to account for ~3% of India’s total electricity consumption by 2030, up from ~1% today underscoring their rising significance as a demand driver.
From an infrastructure perspective, India already hosts 
~150 operational data centers with 1.2–1.5 GW capacity, having attracted investments of approximately USD 6.5 billion over the past decade. Policy support remains a key enabler, with the government granting infrastructure status to the sector and introducing incentives such as a 21-year tax holiday for foreign cloud service providers in Budget 2026–27, alongside favourable transfer pricing frameworks. A long-term policy roadmap extending to 2047 further reinforces India’s ambition to become a global hub for AI and cloud infrastructure.
Importantly, data centers are highly energy-intensive – 
often consuming power comparable to large industrial facilities or up to 1,00,000 households per facility – creating localized, high-density load centers. This not only drives incremental demand but also necessitates reliable, high-quality and increasingly green power solutions.
Consequently, the sector is opening multi-dimensional opportunities across

Overall, the expansion of data centers is reshaping the 
demand profile of the power sector – combining scale, reliability requirements and sustainability imperatives – thereby positioning it as a key structural growth lever in India’s energy transition.

Industry
The industrial growth in India too is a key driver of energy consumption in India. The demand is clearly rising. A 
report from IEA Electricity 2026, suggests that while India’s electricity demand is expected to grow about 6.4% on an average every year until 2030, industry will contribute one third of this demand.
Exports: Expanding regional and global footprint
India is increasingly positioning itself as a global hub 
for power equipment manufacturing, engineering services and project execution. Export opportunities are expanding across key segments such as transformers, grid technologies and EPC services, supported by strong cost competitiveness, a skilled workforce and steadily improving technological capabilities.
In parallel, cross-border power trade is gaining 
momentum, strengthening India’s role within the regional energy ecosystem. Power exports to neighboring countries are fostering energy security, enhancing grid integration and creating a more interconnected South Asian power market. Collectively, these trends are enabling India to expand its global footprint while unlocking incremental growth avenues for the sector.
Services: Lifecycle value creation
Beyond core infrastructure development, services are 
emerging as a critical and growing component of the power sector’s value pool. Lifecycle services – including operations and maintenance (O&M), digital monitoring, predictive analytics, performance optimization and asset management – are gaining increasing traction.
As utilities and developers focus on maximizing efficiency, 
reliability and returns over the asset life cycle, service-led engagement models are becoming more prominent. This shift not only drives recurring revenue streams but also deepens customer relationships and improves long-term asset performance, making services a key strategic lever in the evolving power ecosystem.Power trading: Market evolution and efficiency
Power trading is playing an increasingly important role 
in enhancing market efficiency and balancing supply-demand dynamics. The continued growth of power exchanges, real-time markets and ancillary service mechanisms is enabling better price discovery, improved liquidity and optimized grid operations.
With rising renewable energy penetration, the need for 
flexible and dynamic trading frameworks is becoming more critical. These mechanisms help manage intermittency, ensure grid stability and facilitate the efficient integration of diverse energy sources. As the market matures, power trading is set to become a central pillar in driving efficiency and resilience across the energy system.
Policy and regulatory environment: driving energy 
transition and sector transformation
The Indian power sector continues to witness strong 
policy momentum, with the Government implementing a comprehensive set of reforms and targeted initiatives in FY 2025–26 to accelerate the energy transition, strengthen infrastructure and enhance sector efficiency. These measures are collectively shaping a more resilient, sustainable and self-reliant power ecosystem.
Renewable energy and energy transition remain at the 
core of policy focus. Capacity additions in renewable energy have remained robust, with clean sources contributing a dominant share of incremental capacity. Key government programs such as PM Surya Ghar: Muft Bijli Yojana (for rooftop solar adoption) and the 700TWh

PM-KUSUM Scheme (for solarization of agriculture) are 
driving distributed renewable growth, while the Solar Parks Scheme continues to enable large-scale utility projects. The PLI Scheme for High Efficiency Solar PV Modules is strengthening domestic manufacturing capabilities and initiatives such as PM JANMAN are expanding solar access to underserved and remote communities, supporting inclusive energy access.
To further reinforce the ecosystem, the Government has 
launched the National Manufacturing Mission for Clean Technologies, aimed at building domestic capabilities across solar modules, batteries, wind equipment and electrolysers. This policy push aligns with the broader objective of reducing import dependence, strengthening supply chain resilience and supporting India’s long-term clean energy ambitions.
Energy storage and supply chain strengthening represent 
a critical next phase in the transition. Policy measures such as the extension of Basic Customs Duty (BCD) exemptions for Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS) are expected to reduce capital costs and accelerate deployment of grid-scale storage. This is essential to manage renewable intermittency, improve grid stability and enable higher penetration of clean energy.
Transmission and distribution reforms continue to be a key 
priority area. The Revamped Distribution Sector Scheme (RDSS), with enhanced allocation of approximately ` 18,000 Crores in FY 2025-26, is supporting:
 • Reduction of Aggregate Technical & Commercial (AT&C) losses
 • Large-scale rollout of smart metering
 • Financial and operational turnaround of DISCOMs
In addition, the Government is incentivizing states through reform-linked borrowing mechanisms, while significantly scaling investments in inter-state and intra-state transmission networks to facilitate renewable evacuation. These interventions are improving operational efficiency, enhancing transparency and strengthening reliability across the distribution segment.
At a structural level, reforms such as the Draft National 
Electricity Policy (NEP) 2026 and ongoing distribution sector reforms (including proposed amendments to the Electricity Act) are providing a long-term roadmap for the sector. These focus on resource adequacy planning, tariff rationalization, reduction of cross-subsidies and deeper integration of renewable energy and storage, while promoting competition and improving service quality.
Decarbonization and emerging technologies are being 
actively supported through flagship initiatives such as the National Green Hydrogen Mission, incentives for biogas and alternative fuels and an enhanced focus on nuclear energy, including the development of Small Modular Reactors (SMRs) with potential private sector participation. These initiatives aim to create clean baseload capacity and build future-ready energy systems aligned with India’s long-term climate goals.
Thermal sector reforms and energy security continue to 
evolve under the revised SHAKTI Policy, which enhances flexibility in coal allocation and broadens eligibility for linkages. This is improving the availability of domestic coal, reducing dependence on imports and exposure to global price volatility and ensuring more stable and cost-efficient baseload generation.
Finally, foundational reforms and sector progress 
– including flagship programs such as DDUGJY and SAUBHAGYA – have enabled near-universal electrification, significantly improving access to power and reducing supply deficits. These efforts continue to underpin demand growth and support overall sector stability.
Overall, the policy environment 
remains strongly supportive, with a clear emphasis on clean energy transition, infrastructure strengthening, financial sustainability and energy security, creating a robust foundation for sustained growth in the power sector.
Opportunities and threats
The Indian power sector presents significant 
growth opportunities, underpinned by strong policy support, structural demand drivers and the ongoing energy transition.
•
 Ener
gy transition and renewable integration
 
 India
’s commitment to achieving 900 GW of non-
fossil capacity by 2035 and net-zero emissions by 
2070 is driving large-scale investments in renewable energy. This transition is creating strong demand for advanced grid integration solutions, including HVDC, FACTS and grid automation technologies, as the share of intermittent renewable energy increases.
•
 E
xpansion and modernization of transmission 
infrastructure
 
 Con
tinued investments in Green Energy Corridors, 
interstate transmission expansion and grid strengthening initiatives are driving sustained demand for high-voltage transmission systems. Additionally, aging infrastructure and rising peak demand are accelerating opportunities in digital substations, flexible transmission and grid upgrades.
168
Integrated Annual Report 2025-26w 
=== page 172 ===
• Energy storage and grid flexibility
  Increasing renewable penetration is creating a 
structural need for battery energy storage systems 
(BESS), pumped storage and grid balancing 
solutions. Policy support, including incentives 
and duty rationalization, is expected to accelerate 
adoption, opening new avenues in hybrid solutions 
and grid stabilization technologies.
• Digitalization of power networks
  Utilities are increasingly adopting smart grid 
technologies, advanced analytics and real-time 
monitoring systems to enhance efficiency and 
reduce losses. This is driving demand for software-
led solutions, automation and lifecycle services, 
enabling long-term value creation.
• Electrification, urbanization and emerging demand 
segments
  Rapid urbanization, industrial expansion and 
rising per capita consumption continue to drive 
electricity demand. New segments such as EV 
stands for electric vehicle not electric mobility. 
Either keep electric mobility or electric vehicle, rail 
electrification, metro systems and data centres are 
creating high-growth, quality-sensitive demand for 
reliable power infrastructure.
• Localization and manufacturing opportunities
  Government initiatives such as PLI schemes 
and the National Manufacturing Mission for 
Clean Technologies are strengthening domestic 
manufacturing ecosystems across solar, batteries 
and grid equipment. This supports localization, 
improves cost competitiveness and enhances 
export potential.
• New energy vectors and decarbonization 
technologies
  Emerging areas such as green hydrogen, nuclear 
energy (including SMRs) and carbon markets 
are creating long-term growth opportunities and 
expanding the sector’s addressable market.
Turning challenges into innovation opportunities
Sector challenges such as renewable intermittency, 
transmission losses and grid inefficiencies are creating 
opportunities for smart grid solutions, storage 
technologies and advanced energy management 
systems, enabling innovation-led growth.
Threats
Despite strong growth in drivers, the sector faces 
structural and emerging challenges that may impact 
execution and profitability:• DISCOM financial stress
  Persistent issues such as high AT&C losses, delayed 
payments and tariff distortions continue to affect 
liquidity across the value chain, posing counterparty 
and receivables risks.
• Regulatory and policy uncertainty
  Evolving regulatory frameworks, tariff mechanisms 
and tendering processes may create uncertainty 
in project execution and investment planning, 
impacting timelines and returns.
• Project execution and counterparty risks
  Large infrastructure projects are exposed to land 
acquisition delays, permitting challenges and 
contractor performance risks. The financial condition 
of utilities may also affect project realization.
• Supply chain bottlenecks
  Limited availability of critical components like steel, 
copper, bushings, semiconductors etc can delay 
planned capacity additions, impacting revenue 
and margins
• Renewable intermittency and grid stability risks
  The growing share of renewable energy introduces 
challenges in grid balancing, forecasting and 
storage availability, requiring significant investments 
in flexibility and system resilience.
• Foreign exchange and macroeconomic risks
  Fluctuations in currency, interest rates and inflation 
may impact project economics and profitability, 
particularly for projects involving imported 
components or global contracts.
• Thermal transition and energy security challenges
  While coal remains critical for baseload supply, 
evolving environmental norms and decarbonization 
pressures may require additional investments and 
asset realignment.
• Cybersecurity and digital risks
  Increasing digitalization exposes grid infrastructure to 
cybersecurity threats and operational vulnerabilities, 
making system resilience a critical priority.
• Talent and capability constraints
  The transition toward digital and advanced 
technologies requires specialized skills in 
engineering, analytics and project execution and 
talent shortages may pose execution challenges.
India represents a high-growth, structurally attractive 
market driven by electrification, renewable integration 
and grid modernization. While the long-term outlook 
remains robust, effective management of regulatory 
complexity, execution challenges and financial risks will 
be critical to delivering sustainable and profitable growth.

Outlook 12,13,14
Overall, the sector is well-positioned for sustained long-
term growth, underpinned by a balanced focus on energy security, clean energy transition and infrastructure development, reinforcing its role as a key enabler of economic growth and sustainability
Despite economic headwinds, the electrification of 
transport, industry and cooling is expected to continue driving growth in electricity demand. Policymakers and energy stakeholders will address supply security, pricing volatility and grid stability to sustain the momentum towards global energy transition goals.
The World Energy Outlook 2025 (WEO-2025) warns 
that energy security is now inseparable from geopolitics, supply chain resilience and climate stability. It also stated that emerging economies, particularly India and Asia, will shape the next era of global demand.
Renewable sources of electricity generation continue to 
grow strongly, with global capacity expected to more than double by CY 2030. In 80% of the countries worldwide, renewable power capacity is set to grow faster between CY 2025 and CY 2030 than in the previous five-year period. Renewables are expected to meet over 90% of global electricity demand growth, becoming the largest source of electricity generation globally and contributing 46% of global electricity by CY 2030.
In India, the ongoing expansion of renewable energy 
capacity is supported by long-term policy measures and infrastructure planning initiatives. The Government’s plans for a transmission network capable of supporting 900 GW of non-fossil fuel capacity by FY 2035-36 reflect the scale of the country’s energy transition ambitions. As renewable energy penetration increases, maintaining grid stability, minimizing renewable energy curtailment and managing system-level challenges such as low-frequency oscillations will assume greater importance, necessitating continued investments in transmission infrastructure, grid modernization, energy storage and advanced grid management technologies.
Marked by volatile fossil fuel prices and a rapid transition 
in renewables, the global energy sector impacts India by straining its import-dependent economy. It is simultaneously driving an ambitious, rapid expansion of domestic renewable energy capacity. As the world’s third-largest oil consumer, India continues to benefit from the shifting global clean energy investments, while facing high inflation risks from price shocks.
India is not just increasing its energy capacity, it’s reshaping 
it, tackling climate change head-on while ensuring energy security and providing modern energy access to all. India’s energy journey over the past decade reflects a strategic shift towards self-reliance, sustainability and innovation. With bold reforms, massive infrastructure expansion and a strong push for renewables, the country is not only meeting its growing energy demands but also contributing meaningfully to global climate goals.
From green hydrogen to solar rooftops, rural 
electrification to digital procurement, every initiative underscores India’s vision of inclusive, secure and clean energy for all. As the nation moves forward, its balanced and forward-looking energy strategy continues to power economic growth and a sustainable future.
India’s power sector outlook is underpinned by:
 •Per capita demand catch-up: At ~1.42  MWh, India’s 
p
er capita consumption is well below global levels 
(~3.75
 
MWh), suggesting potential to nearly double 
by 2035 as demand rises.
 •Tech-led growth: EVs and AI/data center applications 
could add tens of TWh to demand by CY 2030–32.
 •Green energy targets: With a 500  GW renewables 
ambition 
by CY 2030, India will increasingly pivot 
to clean sources, improving energy security and emissions profile.
Overall, the sector is well-positioned for sustained long-term growth, underpinned by a balanced focus on energy security, clean energy transition and infrastructure development, reinforcing its role as a key enabler of economic growth and sustainability
Company overview:
Hitachi Energy India Limited (referred to as “the 
Company”) is a subsidiary of Hitachi Energy Ltd., headquartered in Switzerland, a global technology leader committed to advancing the energy transition through innovative solutions in electrification, grid infrastructure and digitalization.
The Company is among India’s leading power technology 
players, serving utility, industry and infrastructure customers through a comprehensive portfolio of products, projects and services across the power value chain. Incorporated in 2019 and headquartered in Bengaluru, it supports the transmission, distribution and integration of electricity through technologies that enable a more sustainable, flexible and secure energy system.
Hitachi Energy is committed to making the world’s 
energy system more sustainable, secure, resilient and affordable, enabling greater access to electricity and empowering societies to thrive. As the Company stands

at the forefront of the emerging electricity era, it is aligned 
with the global movement “The Next 25,” reflecting a

energy systems being built today.
With 19 manufacturing facilities across 8 locations in 
India, supported by 13 sales offices and 8 additional 
sales touchpoints and an employee strength of 2,777 
(as on March 31, 2026), the Company delivers 
innovative solutions across the utility, industry and 
infrastructure sectors.
The Company offers wide range of products and services 
for energy networks. Its solutions ensure reliable and 
high-quality power supply across transmission networks, 
utilities, transport systems, renewable energy, data 
centers and industrial applications.
In India, the Company powers 9 out of 18 HVDC links, 
while its transformers support 1 in 3 Indian Railways 
locomotives. With market-leading positions across its 
business units, its technologies connect over 60 GW 
of renewable energy and approximately 80% of metro 
systems in the country operate using its solutions. The 
Company continues to drive innovation by pioneering 
advanced technologies while maintaining and 
modernizing one of the world’s largest installed bases.
The Company operates on a sustainable business 
model, leveraging advanced technologies and digital 
transformation to support customers in achieving their 
Net Zero targets. It plays a key role in enhancing the 
efficiency of India’s energy value chain and improving 
energy accessibility, contributing to the nation’s transition 
toward a carbon-neutral future. With a strong customer-
centric approach, the Company focuses on profitable 
growth by continuously upgrading existing offerings 
and introducing new products aligned with evolving 
market needs.
Business segments
Core segments
The Company operates through five business units: Grid 
Automation, Grid Integration, Transformers, High Voltage 
Products and Service. It serves a diverse customer base 
across renewables, transmission, utilities, data centers and 
railways. By leveraging Service, Exports and Digitalization 
as key growth drivers, while maintaining a strong focus 
on operational efficiency, the Company continues to 
enhance productivity, strengthen quality standards and 
expand its presence in high-growth segments.
Customer-centric approach
Customer centricity remains a cornerstone of the 
Company’s strategy. Guided by collaboration and co-
creation, Hitachi Energy works closely with customers 
and partners to advance sustainable energy solutions 
across the value chain.The Company actively engages with stakeholders through 
customer interactions and knowledge-sharing platforms 
designed to strengthen relationships, gather feedback 
and enhance overall customer experience. Its front-end 
sales teams, supported by technical experts, deliver 
tailored solutions and project support across segments.
The Company also demonstrates its thought leadership 
and technological capabilities at leading industry 
platforms such as the India AI Impact Summit, Bharat 
Electricity Summit and India Energy Week. Strategic 
engagement is further strengthened through leadership 
interactions, customer workshops and on-site seminars, 
enabling the Company to address evolving technology 
needs and deliver impactful, solution-oriented outcomes.
Operational performance
The Company remained focused on enhancing its overall 
efficiency across all spheres of work and maintained 
a sharp focus on improving customer experience 
through robust project implementation. The recently 
commissioned India's first HVDC city center infeed 
in Mumbai is a testament to the Company’s strong 
on-time execution. A strong order backlog and long-
term planning have sustained the Company's growth 
momentum amid the volatile geopolitical landscape. 
The Company effectively navigated the impact on the 
industry from the recent global energy price shocks.
The year witnessed a surge in total order backlog with an 
order book of ` 29,555.3 Crores as of March 31, 2026, 
reflecting the evolving energy landscape worldwide and 
providing revenue visibility for several quarters. The 
order book was led by orders for HVDC control system 
refurbishment, grid connection solutions and transformer 
and disconnector supply. In terms of segments, the 
major contributors were rail, metro and data centers. 
The Service segment contributed 71% to the total order 
book, with key service orders received from state utilities, 
data centers, transmission and industry for system 
refurbishment, SCADA upgrades and life cycle services.
Exports contributed 30.3% to the 
total order book (w/o HVDC), as the 
Company received export orders from 
the US, Europe and APAC. During the 
year, the Board approved an investment 
of ` 2,000 Crores to set up a greenfield 
large power transformers facility in 
Karjan, Vadodara, Gujarat, taking the 
cumulative capex to ` 4,000 Crores.

Sustainability
Hitachi Energy India Limited is at the forefront of the clean 
energy tech and digital-led revolution. Your Company’s focus and targeted actions towards sustainability enabled it to consolidate its position as a leader in this sector. Your Company has set specific targets on four key areas to be achieved by 2030 – Planet, People, Peace and Partnerships.
For FY 2025-26, the Company has taken definitive 
measures which are in line with its 2030 sustainability goals. Several initiatives have been taken on emissions reduction, energy and water conservation and waste reduction. The Company achieved a 74% reduction in CO2 emissions and an 82% reduction in waste disposed to landfill or incineration from the baseline year of 2019. Also, 99% of the waste produced has been processed for recycling in the same financial year.
The Company’s Halol manufacturing facility has been 
certified as Water Positive following independent verification of its Water Positive Index for FY 2025-26. Additionally, the facilities at Halol and Mysore have achieved the "Platinum Level" certification for Zero Waste to Landfill.
The Company’s sustainability strategy, which consists of 
three strands of action, People, Planet and Principles, provides guidance for the future years. The Company has set sustainability targets and commitments for 2030 and the roadmap for transition to Net Zero by 2050.
Industry collaborations
The Company actively participated in key industry 
forums, policy dialogues and sectoral summits during the year, contributing to discussions on energy transition, renewable integration, grid modernization, power transmission reforms and the growing energy requirements of emerging technologies such as artificial intelligence and data centers. Through these engagements, the Company continued to collaborate with policymakers, utilities, industry bodies and other stakeholders to support the development of a sustainable and reliable energy ecosystem.
License to operate: Safety, Integrity & Quality
At Hitachi Energy India Limited, safety remains paramount 
and is deeply ingrained in the organization’s DNA. The Company is committed to fostering a strong safety culture across all functions and will continue to reinforce this commitment through consistent actions. Upholding the highest standards of safety, integrity and quality, Hitachi Energy actively emphasizes the importance of timely reporting of safety incidents across its work sites.
The Company has refreshed its Health, Safety and 
Environment (HSE) policy to further promote employee well-being, ensure safe working conditions and drive positive health outcomes. It continues to strengthen its safety practices across all its manufacturing locations and offices, achieving 100% on-time closure of high-risk hazard situations.
During the year, the Company reported a Total 
Recordable Injury Frequency Rate (TRIFR) of 0.11, reflecting its sustained focus and disciplined execution of safety initiatives.
“Integrity remains the cornerstone of our business 
principles and operational excellence. The Company is committed to upholding the highest standards of ethical conduct, transparency and compliance across all areas. The Hitachi Code of Ethics and Business Conduct continue to be reinforced through onboarding programs, annual refresher trainings and engaging awareness initiatives. I. COMPANY’S PHILOSOPHY ON CODE OF GOVERNANCE: 
 Your Company’s Corporate Governance philosophy is to build and manage sustainable businesses anchored by 
the highest standards of honesty, transparency and accountability. Your Company aim to maximize stakeholder 
value while strictly adhering to all applicable laws and regulations. Your Company believe that effective 
leadership, robust policies, processes and system and a rich legacy of values form the hallmark of a world-class 
governance framework.
 The Company fully realizes its Shareholders’ entitlement to information on the Company’s performance and 
considers itself a trustee of its Shareholders. The Company provides thorough information to its Shareholders on a 
variety of subjects impacting the Company’s business and financial performance. The Company’s basic corporate 
governance concept is to achieve business excellence and devote itself to growing long-term Shareholder value 
while keeping all stakeholders’ requirements and interests in mind. The Company is dedicated to transparency 
in all of its dealings and values corporate ethics. Good corporate governance is the basis for decision-making 
and control processes.

  As of March 31, 2026, the Board comprises six (6) Directors, maintaining an optimal balance of executive 
and non-executive expertise. The composition is in full compliance with Regulation 17 of the SEBI (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (‘SEBI Listing Regulations’):
 • Independent Directors: Three (3) Directors, including two (2) Women Independent Directors.
 • Non-Independent Directors: Three (3) Directors, comprising:
   - One (1) Managing Director & CEO
   - Two (2) Non-Executive Directors (Nominees of the Promoter*)
   *The Promoter, Hitachi Energy Ltd. (formerly known as Hitachi ABB Power Grids Ltd), holds 71.31% of the paid-up 
equity share capital of the Company and is represented by two Nominee Directors
  The Board composition is in conformity with the applicable provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 (‘the 
Act’) and the SEBI Listing Regulations. The Chairman of the Board is a Non-Executive and Non-Independent 
Director. None of the Directors on the Board are related to each other (inter-se). Their professional 
relationship is strictly limited to the receipt of sitting fees, commission and reimbursement of expenses 
incurred for attending Company Meetings. A clear distinction exists between the roles and duties of the 
Chairman and that of the MD & CEO.
  In accordance with the provisions of the Act, the Managing Director and Independent Directors are not 
liable to retire by rotation. The remaining two Non-Executive, Non-Independent Directors are liable to 
retire by rotation. Accordingly, at the ensuing Annual General Meeting (‘AGM’), Mr. Jan Niklas Persson  
(DIN: 11239092), being eligible, has offered himself for re-appointment.

 ( g)  Boar d’s Core Skills/ Expertise/ Competencies:
 
 F
or effective functioning of the Board, the Company’s Board needs to have skills/expertise/competencies 
in the areas of Business, Finance & Accounting, Governance/Legal and Leadership. Accordingly, for the 
effective discharge of its roles and responsibilities, the Board of the Company is comprised of Directors possessing an appropriate mix of skills, expertise and competencies in the areas of Business, Finance & Accounting, Governance/Legal and Leadership, relevant to the Company’s business and governance requirements. The Board collectively brings together a diverse range of professional experience, enabling informed decisionmaking and effective oversight.
 
 The Dir
ectors come from varied professional backgrounds and geographies, bringing global perspectives 
and sectoral insights that support the Company’s longterm growth. The Board as a whole possesses the requisite qualifications, experience, independence and competencies necessary to meet the Company’s governance standards.

 (h)
 Succession Planning f
or the Board and Senior Management:
 
 The 
Company has a well-defined and robust system in place to ensure smooth transitions in leadership, 
including for our Directors, Executive Director, Senior Management Team and other critical talents and 
key roles.
 
 This 
is an annual global process where employees across all levels are rated on their Potential Index in the 9 
Box grid, retention risk loss impact and successors for key positions are identified. Additionally, the Company regularly reviews talents for Senior Management and other executive officers. This process provides a comprehensive overview of our leadership level talent inventory and capabilities, ensuring that we meet our critical talent needs in alignment with our business drivers. The aforementioned is also reviewed by the Nomination & Remuneration Committee annually and recommended to the Board.

 (m) Board Committees:
  In accordance with the provisions of the applicable laws, the Board has constituted various Committees and 
delegated specific powers and responsibilities to them to facilitate focused and efficient decisionmaking. 
Each Committee operates within its defined terms of reference and undertakes detailed deliberations on 
matters within its purview before placing its recommendations before the Board.
  The Committees function under the overall supervision of the Board and are empowered to make informed 
decisions within the scope of the authority delegated to them. Committee meetings are generally held prior 
to the Board Meetings and the Chairperson of each Committee apprises the Board of the deliberations, 
decisions taken and recommendations made by the Committee. All such recommendations and decisions 
are placed before the Board either for approval or for information, as applicable. During the financial year 
under review, all recommendations made by the Committees were accepted by the Board.
  The following statutory Committees have been constituted by the Board in compliance with the provisions 
of the Act and the SEBI Listing Regulations:
 • Audit Committee
 • Nomination and Remuneration Committee
 • Stakeholders’ Relationship Committee
 • Risk Management Committee
 • Corporate Social Responsibility Committee
  Further, the Board from time to time, based on necessity, has also delegated few other operational powers 
to certain Committees namely:
 • Environmental, Social and Governance Committee
 • Fund Raise Committee
2. Audit Committee:
(i) Terms of Reference: 
 In accordance with the provisions of Section 177 of the Act and Regulation 18 of the SEBI Listing Regulations, 
the Company has formed its Audit Committee, composition and terms of reference of which are in conformity 
with the said provisions. 
 The terms of reference are briefly described below:
 • Oversight of the Company’s financial reporting process and the disclosure of its financial information to 
ensure that the financial statement is correct, sufficient and credible. 
 • Recommending the Board, appointment, re-appointment, replacement or removal (in the event of necessity) 
of Statutory Auditors, Cost Auditors, Secretarial Auditors and/ or any other auditors including terms of their 
appointment and fixation of remuneration. 
 • Approval of payment to Statutory Auditors for any other services rendered by the Statutory Auditors. 
 • Reviewing, with the Management, the annual financial statements and auditor’s report thereon before 
submission to the Board for approval, with particular reference to:- 
  (a) Matters required to be included in the Director’s Responsibility Statement to be included in the Board’s 
Report in terms of clause (c) of sub-section (3) of Section 134 of the Act. 
  (b) Changes, if any, in accounting policies and practices and reasons for the same. 
  (c) Major accounting entries involving estimates based on the exercise of judgment by Management. 
  (d) Significant adjustments made in the financial statements arising out of audit findings.

  (e) Compliance with listing and other legal requirements relating to financial statements.
  (f) Disclosure of any related party transactions; and

suspected fraud or irregularity or a failure of internal control systems of a material nature and reporting the 
matter to the Board.

as well as post-audit discussion to ascertain any area of concern. 
 • To look into the reasons for substantial defaults in the payment to the depositors, debenture holders, 
Shareholders (in case of non-payment of declared dividends) and creditors. 
 • Review the functioning of the Whistle Blower mechanism.

 T
erms of Reference:
 T
he terms of reference and role of the Nomination and Remuneration Committee (‘NRC’) are as per the provisions 
of Section 178 of the Act and Regulation 19 of the SEBI Listing Regulations which includes formulating the 
criteria to:
 • Determine qualifications, positive attributes and independence of a director and recommend to the Board a Policy, relating to the remuneration of the directors, KMP and other employees.
 • For every appointment of an Independent Director, the NRC shall evaluate the balance of skills, knowledge and experience on the Board and on the basis of such evaluation, prepare a description of the role and capabilities required of an Independent Director. The person recommended to the Board for appointment as an Independent Director shall have the capabilities identified in such description.  • Recommending to the Board on remuneration, Performance Bonus etc., payable to the Executive Director(s) / Managing Director, Commission payable to Independent Directors.

(iii) Remuneration Policy / Criteria for Payments to Directors / Senior Management Employees:
 The Company has a credible and transparent Policy in determining and accounting for the remuneration of 
the Executive / Non-Executive Independent Directors. Independent Directors’ remuneration is governed by 
the external competitive environment, track record of the individuals, effective participation in the Meetings, 
comparable industry standards and performance of the Company. The remuneration determined for the Executive 
/ Independent Directors is subject to the recommendation of the NRC and approval of the Board.  
 The Nomination and Remuneration Policy covers the following aspects:
 • Lays down the criteria and terms and conditions with regard to identifying persons who are qualified to 
become Directors (Executive and Non-Executive) and persons who may be appointed at Senior Management 
and Key Managerial positions and to determine their remuneration.
 • Formulation of criteria for evaluation of the performance of the Board, KMP and Senior Management 
Personnel as required under the law.
 The Board and the NRC also reviewed the performance of the Board on the basis of the criteria such as the 
contribution of the individual director to the Board and Committee Meetings like preparedness on the issues to 
be discussed, meaningful and constructive contribution and inputs in Meetings, etc. In addition, the Chairman 
was also evaluated on the key aspects of his role.
 The Board, based on the recommendation of the NRC has adopted the performance review criteria. After the 
conclusion of the period under review, the revision was carried out by the NRC in the performance evaluation 
criteria. Subsequently, the NRC and the Board have reviewed the performance of the Directors and the Board 
as a whole for the financial year under review.
 The Non-Executive Independent Directors were compensated by way of commission for the financial year  
2025-26 in the financial year 2026-27 and the criteria being their attendance in the Board / Committee 
Meetings, apart from sitting fees and reimbursement of other expenses as approved by the Shareholders at the 
3rd AGM held on July 22, 2022.
 As per Policy, the Non-Executive Non-Independent Directors are neither paid a sitting fee nor paid any 
commission. 
 The Independent Directors are entitled to sitting fees for attending the Board/Committee Meetings. 
 Sitting fees for Board and Audit Committee Meetings is `  60,000/- per Director per Meeting and for other 
Committees, the sitting fee is ` 30,000/- per Director per Meeting. 
 The remuneration payable to the Managing Director and CEO and Senior Management personnel including 
KMP are structured as fixed and variable components. The fixed remuneration comprises salaries, perquisites 
and retirement benefits and the variable component comprises annual performance bonus which is linked to 
the achievement of the scorecard fixed at the beginning of the year and Long Term Incentive Plan (‘LTIP’) which 
is subject to achievement of performance measures as attached to the Grant Letter issued from time to time in 
line with Hitachi Energy group Policy.
